````markdown
 64: **Fire condition:** 2+ "**Label:** explanation" bullets in the same list.
 65: 
 66: **Exclusion:** Legitimate reference content — API parameter tables, changelog entries, feature comparison grids — where label-and-description is the correct and expected format.
 67: 
 68: **Burstiness note:** When rewriting, avoid converting every bullet into a single run-on sentence. Vary the approach: some items can merge into prose, others can become numbered steps if order matters, and short items can stay as a plain list without bold labels.
 69: 
 70: **Problem:** AI converts prose into pseudo-heading bullet points with a bold label and colon on every item. This format is appropriate for reference docs but not for articles, essays, or narrative text.
 71: 
 72: **Semantic Risk:** LOW
 73: 
 74: **Before:**
 75: > - **Accessibility:** The platform supports screen readers and keyboard navigation.
 76: > - **Performance:** Load times have been reduced by 40%.
 77: > - **Security:** All data is encrypted end-to-end.
 78: 
 79: **After:**
 80: > The platform now supports screen readers and keyboard navigation, load times are down 40%, and all data is encrypted end-to-end.
 81: 
 82: ---
 83: 
 84: ### 16. Title Case in Headings
 85: 
 86: **Fire condition:** 3+ content words (non-articles, non-prepositions) capitalized in the same heading when sentence case would be correct.
 87: 
 88: **Exclusion:** Proper nouns, brand names, product names, and acronyms within headings always require capitalization regardless of style guide.
 89: 
 90: **Problem:** AI capitalizes every word in headings (Title Case) instead of using sentence case. In most modern style guides (AP, APA, Google developer docs), sentence case is preferred for readability.
 91: 
 92: **Semantic Risk:** LOW
 93: 
 94: **Before:**
 95: > ## The Impact Of Remote Work On Urban Development And Housing Markets
 96: 
 97: **After:**
 98: > ## The impact of remote work on urban development and housing markets
 99: 
100: ---
101: 
102: ### 17. Emojis
103: 
104: **Watch words:** emojis used as section markers or emphasis
105: 
106: **Fire condition:** Any emoji in professional, academic, or editorial text.
107: 
108: **Exclusion:** Personal blogs, social media copy, and casual newsletters where emojis are a deliberate stylistic choice. Also exclude emoji in quoted material being analyzed.
109: 
110: **Problem:** AI inserts emojis to add visual interest to text where they are inappropriate. This is especially common in listicles, summaries, and instructional content.
111: 
112: **Semantic Risk:** LOW
113: 
114: **Before:**
115: > Here are five tips for better sleep:
116: > 1. Set a consistent bedtime
117: > 2. Avoid screens before bed
118: > 3. Keep your room cool
119: > 4. Limit caffeine after noon
120: > 5. Try reading instead of scrolling
121: 
122: **After:**
123: > Five tips for better sleep:
124: > 1. Set a consistent bedtime.
125: > 2. Avoid screens before bed.
126: > 3. Keep your room cool.
127: > 4. Limit caffeine after noon.
128: > 5. Read instead of scrolling.
129: 
130: ---
131: 
132: ### 18. Curly Quotation Marks
133: 
134: **Watch words:** curly/smart quotes used in contexts where straight quotes are standard
135: 
136: **Fire condition:** Curly quotes appear inside code blocks, inline code spans, configuration file examples, or plain-text technical documents where straight quotes are required.
137: 
138: **Exclusion:** Curly quotes in narrative prose are typographically correct and not this pattern. Only trigger in technical or code contexts where curly quotes cause syntax errors or compatibility issues.
139: 
140: **Problem:** AI generates curly quotes in contexts where straight quotes are standard, such as code blocks, plain-text documents, configuration files, and technical documentation. This can cause syntax errors and compatibility issues.
141: 
142: **Semantic Risk:** LOW
143: 
144: **Before:**
145: > Set the variable: `name = "hello"`
146: >
147: > In your config file, add: `timeout = '30'`
148: 
149: **After:**
150: > Set the variable: `name = "hello"`
151: >
152: > In your config file, add: `timeout = '30'`
````

## File: patterns/en-viral-hook.md
````markdown
  1: ---
  2: pack: en-viral-hook
  3: language: en
  4: name: Viral Hook Patterns
  5: version: 1.0.0
  6: patterns: 9
  7: corpus-snapshot:
  8:   id: bootstrap-patterns-pre-provenance
  9:   status: needs-quarterly-refresh
 10:   source: maintainer-curated pattern packs before quarterly corpus snapshot tracking
 11:   last_validated: null
 12: score_only: true
 13: ---
 14: 
 15: # Viral Hook Patterns (score-only)
 16: 
 17: This pack is **score- and audit-only**. It catches "AI marketing influencer" signals — shock-number hooks, clickbait closings, source-skipping authority claims, breath-optimized short-sentence stacking, hyperbolic engagement vocabulary, fake-stat citations, stacked credentials, future-self promises, and aphoristic standalone punchlines — that are common in SNS and blog marketing copy. Rewrite mode does not touch them because they may be intentional rhetoric; the user decides.
 18: 
 19: A hit here does not mean the text is AI-generated. Humans use these patterns too. But when several appear together, the score aligns with the reader's intuition that "this reads like AI-polished marketing."
 20: 
 21: ---
 22: 
 23: ### 1. Shock Numbers as Hook
 24: 
 25: **Watch words:** in just N days, only N hours, jumped from 0 to N, hit N million in N weeks, N% growth in N months, broke the N-million mark, N-figure result
 26: 
 27: **Problem:** Specific shock numbers are used as the primary impact lever. Without a verifiable source (link, screenshot, named outlet), this reads more like marketing-bot or AI-influencer copy than something a person wrote about their own experience.
 28: 
 29: **Fire condition:** A bold claim relies on a striking number (time-to-X, scale, percentage) and the same piece offers no source or verification path.
 30: 
 31: **Exclusion:**
 32: - First-person reports of personal numbers ("my salary is $4,000")
 33: - Idioms ("a million times")
 34: - Common-knowledge statistics that are independently verifiable
 35: 
 36: **Semantic Risk:** LOW — score-only, not rewritten.
 37: **Detection examples:**
 38: > 250K stars in just 60 days.
 39: 
 40: > $100M revenue with zero ad spend.
 41: 
 42: ---
 43: 
 44: ### 2. Clickbait Mystery Close
 45: 
 46: **Watch words:** what makes them tick, why is everyone, you'll never guess, can you believe, the reason might surprise you, here's why
 47: 
 48: **Problem:** The piece ends on an unresolved rhetorical question or teaser to drive clicks, follows, and comments instead of delivering information. AI-generated viral copy almost never skips this closing pattern.
 49: 
 50: **Fire condition:** The **last sentence** of the piece is a rhetorical question that the body has not answered, or a teaser that explicitly redirects the reader to engagement (subscribe, follow, save).
 51: 
 52: **Exclusion:**
 53: - Genuine questions that lead into a follow-up paragraph or piece that answers them
 54: - Real invitations to discussion with a specific channel and topic
 55: 
 56: **Semantic Risk:** LOW — score-only.
 57: **Detection examples:**
 58: > So why are devs flocking to it without any marketing?
 59: 
 60: > The reason might surprise you.
 61: 
 62: ---
 63: 
 64: ### 3. Source-Skipping Authority
 65: 
 66: **Watch words:** for the first time in history, a global first, never before seen, the only X that, reportedly (with no named source), industry insiders say (with no source)
 67: 
 68: **Problem:** Absolute authority or scope claims are made without a verifiable source. People typically hedge ("from what I've seen") or attribute; AI-influencer copy goes for impact via plain assertion.
 69: 
 70: **Fire condition:** Absolute scope or rank claims ("first ever", "no one has", "industry-wide change") appear without an accompanying source, link, screenshot, or named expert.
 71: 
 72: **Exclusion:**
 73: - Self-evident factual statements
 74: - Idiomatic exaggeration (clearly hyperbolic for effect)
 75: - First-person personal claims ("the best book I've ever read")
 76: 
 77: **Semantic Risk:** LOW — score-only.
 78: **Detection examples:**
 79: > The fastest growth GitHub has ever seen.
 80: 
 81: > Developers worldwide are losing their minds over this.
 82: 
 83: ---
 84: 
 85: ### 4. Breath-Optimized Short-Sentence Stacking
 86: 
 87: **Watch words:** (structural pattern — judged by form, not vocabulary)
 88: 
 89: **Problem:** Sentences are intentionally clipped to one line each and separated by line breaks for vertical-scroll readability. This is a hallmark of SNS and blog marketing formatting. Real long-form human writing usually has natural burstiness — a mix of short and longer sentences.
 90: 
 91: **Fire condition:** The whole piece is composed almost entirely of one-sentence paragraphs, four or more in a row, with average sentence length under ~12 words. A handful of short sentences mixed with longer ones does not fire this.
 92: 
 93: **Exclusion:**
 94: - Poetry, song lyrics, verse
 95: - Genuinely short notes, announcements, alerts
 96: - One- or two-line answers to a question
 97: - Pieces dominated by code blocks, lists, or quoted dialogue
 98: 
 99: **Semantic Risk:** LOW — score-only; the format may be intentional.
100: **Detection examples:**
101: > No one has ever shipped this fast.
102: >
103: > 250K stars in 60 days.
104: >
105: > A tool called OpenClaw did it.
106: >
107: > Why are devs flocking to it without any marketing?
108: 
109: (Four one-line paragraphs, average ~10 words, line-break separated → fires.)
110: 
111: ---
112: 
113: ### 5. Hyperbolic Engagement Lexicon
114: 
115: **Watch words:** absolutely insane, totally wild, mind-blowing, game-changing, you can't sleep on this, don't miss out, hands down the best, literally everyone is, this changes everything, a no-brainer
116: 
117: **Problem:** Hype vocabulary tuned for SNS engagement. Humans use these too, but several stacked together is a strong signal — and AI-generated viral copy uses them as default amplifiers.
118: 
119: **Fire condition:**
120: - One occurrence in the piece: Low
121: - Two occurrences: Medium
122: - Three or more: High
123: 
124: **Exclusion:**
125: - Joking or self-deprecating context that explicitly flags the exaggeration
126: - Quoted speech or dialogue
127: - Sports, gaming, or other domains where these terms are conventional and a measurable outcome supports them
128: 
129: **Semantic Risk:** LOW — score-only.
130: **Detection examples:**
131: > This is absolutely insane and developers are losing their minds.
132: 
133: > Hands down the best dev tool of the year — don't sleep on it.
134: 
135: ---
136: 
137: ### 6. Fake Statistic Citation
138: 
139: **Watch words:** studies show N%, research says N%, data shows N%, according to research (with no named source), N% of people, science says, survey found (with no survey named)
140: 
141: **Problem:** A precise statistic borrows the feel of research without giving the reader a source to check. AI-polished marketing copy often uses fake precision to make ordinary advice feel proven.
142: 
143: **Fire condition:** A numeric or statistical claim is attributed to vague research, data, surveys, or science, and the same piece gives no named source, link, sample, date, or method.
144: 
145: **Severity rubric:**
146: - Low: One vague statistic supports a minor point.
147: - Medium: The statistic opens the piece or anchors the main call to action.
148: - High: Precise percentages support health, finance, career, or safety advice, or multiple unsourced statistics appear together.
149: 
150: **Exclusion:**
151: - A named report, publication, dataset, or linked source is provided
152: - The number is clearly labeled as an example or hypothetical
153: - First-party analytics include scope and measurement context
154: - Common public data that the reader can verify independently
155: 
156: **Semantic Risk:** LOW — score-only.
157: **Preservation Note:** Score-only by default; if the user asks to lower the hook, preserve real numbers and either cite, hedge, or remove the fake research frame. Do not invent a source.
158: **Before / after examples (manual lower-signal rewrite):**
159: > Before: Studies show 73% of founders lose money because they ignore this one habit.
160: >
161: > After: I do not have a source for that percentage, but weekly cash reviews can catch losses earlier.
162: 
163: ---
164: 
165: ### 7. Manufactured Authority Stacking
166: 
167: **Watch words:** Stanford-trained, Y Combinator-backed, ex-Google, Harvard-trained, Forbes-featured, award-winning, serial founder, trusted by top CEOs, industry-leading expert
168: 
169: **Problem:** Several prestige labels are stacked before a claim so the authority does the persuasive work. This reads like a growth-thread template when the credentials are vague, irrelevant, or not tied to evidence.
170: 
171: **Fire condition:** Two or more prestige credentials, titles, investors, schools, employers, awards, or media labels are stacked to sell a claim, and the piece does not show why those credentials prove the point.
172: 
173: **Severity rubric:**
174: - Low: Two credentials appear, but the person or product is named and the claim is narrow.
175: - Medium: Three or more credentials introduce advice, a product, or a trend without evidence.
176: - High: The credential stack replaces evidence for high-stakes advice or a direct purchase/signup call.
177: 
178: **Exclusion:**
179: - Resume, bio, speaker note, or press boilerplate where credentials are the subject
180: - Named, verifiable credentials that are relevant to the claim
181: - A sourced article where credentials supplement, rather than replace, evidence
182: 
183: **Semantic Risk:** LOW — score-only.
184: **Preservation Note:** Score-only by default; if toned down, keep verifiable affiliations and remove only the pile-up or unsupported leap from title to conclusion.
185: **Before / after examples (manual lower-signal rewrite):**
186: > Before: A Stanford-trained, Y Combinator-backed serial founder says this one workflow will 10x your growth.
187: >
188: > After: A founder shared the workflow they use for weekly growth reviews; test it on your own numbers before adopting it.
189: 
190: ---
191: 
192: ### 8. Future-Self / Parasocial Promise
193: 
194: **Watch words:** your future self will thank you, thank me later, listen friend, friend, save this for later, one year from now you'll be glad, future you, trust me on this
195: 
196: **Problem:** The copy simulates intimacy or future gratitude to pressure the reader into saving, sharing, or obeying advice. It is common in viral threads because it creates emotional urgency without adding evidence.
197: 
198: **Fire condition:** An opener or close addresses the reader as a friend, future self, or intimate confidant and promises later gratitude, usually around a save/share/follow call, without concrete support in the same piece.
199: 
200: **Severity rubric:**
201: - Low: One soft save-this or future-self phrase appears in a casual social post.
202: - Medium: The future-self promise frames the title, opener, or final call to action.
203: - High: The promise is paired with urgency, scarcity, or high-stakes life/career advice.
204: 
205: **Exclusion:**
206: - A real message to a known friend or community member
207: - Coaching, therapy, or accountability writing where the relationship is explicit
208: - Memoir or reflection addressed to the writer's own past/future self
209: - Plain calendar or reminder instructions without emotional pressure
210: 
211: **Semantic Risk:** LOW — score-only.
212: **Preservation Note:** Score-only by default; if rewritten, keep any useful reminder or action while removing simulated intimacy and unverifiable future payoff.
213: **Before / after examples (manual lower-signal rewrite):**
214: > Before: Listen, friend — your future self will thank you for saving this.
215: >
216: > After: Save this if you need a checklist for next month's planning.
217: 
218: ---
219: 
220: ### 9. Aphoristic Punchline / Standalone Declarative
221: 
222: **Watch words:** (structural pattern — judged by form, not vocabulary)
223: 
224: **Problem:** A short, grammatically complete declarative sentence (roughly ten words or fewer) is set on its own line or paragraph for rhetorical gravitas — a pseudo-profound mic-drop. One can be a stylistic choice; several across a piece, or one capping each paragraph, is a hallmark of AI-polished thought-leadership copy that gestures at depth without earning it. The judgment is about form and placement, not the words used.
225: 
226: **Fire condition:** A standalone, grammatically complete declarative sentence of about ten words or fewer is isolated on its own line or paragraph as a punchline, and the piece contains two or more such isolated aphorisms or caps successive paragraphs with them.
227: 
228: **Severity rubric:**
229: - Low: One standalone aphorism appears in the piece.
230: - Medium: Two standalone aphorisms appear.
231: - High: Three or more appear, or nearly every paragraph is capped with one.
232: 
233: **Exclusion:**
234: - Poetry, song lyrics, verse
235: - Genuinely short notes, answers, alerts, or one-line replies
236: - A deliberate aphorism backed by concrete support in the same passage
237: - Quoted speech or dialogue
238: - Headings and section titles
239: 
240: **Semantic Risk:** LOW — score-only; the isolated line may be intentional rhetoric.
241: **Preservation Note:** Score-only by default; if the user asks to lower the hook, fold the standalone line back into the surrounding paragraph or attach the concrete reason it points at, so the claim survives without the staged mic-drop.
242: **Before / after examples (manual lower-signal rewrite):**
243: > Before: We rebuilt the onboarding flow over two sprints.
244: >
245: > Symmetry becomes a trap.
246: >
247: > The team learned to ship smaller.
248: >
249: > Constraints are a gift.
250: >
251: > After: We rebuilt the onboarding flow over two sprints, and matching every screen to the old layout slowed us down — chasing visual symmetry became a trap. The team learned to ship smaller pieces, and the tight scope each sprint actually helped more than it hurt.
````

## File: src/features/index.js
````javascript
  1: // patina-lane: A (deterministic substrate) — LLM-free. See docs/ARCHITECTURE.md.
  2: // Top-level analyzer: runs the deterministic stylometry + lexicon signals
  3: // described in core/stylometry.md and returns a per-paragraph result. This
  4: // is the in-tree port of the algorithm previously delegated to the LLM via
  5: // SKILL.md Step 4.6/4.7. It does not call any LLM.
  6: 
  7: import { splitParagraphs, splitSentences, splitProseSentences, tokenize } from './segment.js';
  8: import {
  9:   burstinessCV,
 10:   mattr,
 11:   classifyBurstiness,
 12:   classifyMattr,
 13:   classifyKoreanDiagnostics,
 14:   koreanDiagnostics,
 15:   commaDensity,
 16:   koreanPosDiversityProxy,
 17:   koreanSpacingFeatures,
 18:   DEFAULT_BURSTINESS_BANDS,
 19:   DEFAULT_KO_DIAGNOSTIC_BANDS,
 20:   DEFAULT_MATTR_BANDS,
 21:   DEFAULT_MATTR_WINDOW,
 22:   DEFAULT_MIN_BURSTINESS_SENTENCES,
 23:   koreanPostEditeseFeatures,
 24:   koreanEndingMonotony,
 25:   DEFAULT_KO_ENDING_MONOTONY,
 26:   detectKoreanRegister,
 27: } from './stylometry.js';
 28: import {
 29:   classifyLexiconHot,
 30:   loadLexicon,
 31:   computeDensity,
 32:   DEFAULT_LEXICON_DENSITY_THRESHOLD,
 33:   DEFAULT_LEXICON_MIN_HOT_MATCHES,
 34: } from './lexicon.js';
 35: import { detectMarkupLeakage } from './markup-leakage.js';
 36: import {
 37:   detectDiscourseTells,
 38:   detectFakeCandor,
 39:   detectThematicBreaks,
 40:   isThematicBreakOnly,
 41: } from './discourse-tells.js';
 42: import { detectTranslationese } from './translationese.js';
 43: import { extractStructuralFeatures, structuralFeatureRecord, STRUCTURAL_FEATURE_NAMES } from './structural-features.js';
 44: import {
 45:   applyScaler,
 46:   fitScaler,
 47:   normalizeStructuralModel,
 48:   predictStructuralScore,
 49:   structuralModelVerdict,
 50:   thresholdForMaxFpr,
 51:   trainLogReg,
 52: } from './structural-classifier.js';
 53: import { loadStructuralModel, resolveStructuralModelPath } from './structural-model-loader.js';
 54: 
 55: export function analyzeText(text, opts = {}) {
 56:   const {
 57:     lang = 'en',
 58:     repoRoot,
 59:     burstinessBands = DEFAULT_BURSTINESS_BANDS,
 60:     minBurstinessSentences = DEFAULT_MIN_BURSTINESS_SENTENCES,
 61:     mattrBands = DEFAULT_MATTR_BANDS,
 62:     mattrWindow = DEFAULT_MATTR_WINDOW,
 63:     koDiagnosticsEnabled = true,
 64:     koDiagnosticBands = DEFAULT_KO_DIAGNOSTIC_BANDS,
 65:     koEndingMonotonyBands = DEFAULT_KO_ENDING_MONOTONY,
 66:     lexiconDensityThreshold = DEFAULT_LEXICON_DENSITY_THRESHOLD,
 67:     lexiconMinHotMatches = DEFAULT_LEXICON_MIN_HOT_MATCHES,
 68:     lexicon: providedLexicon,
 69:     structuralModel = null,
 70:   } = opts;
 71: 
 72:   // Normalize to NFC at the boundary so downstream tokenization and lexicon
 73:   // comparison see canonical form. Mixed NFC/NFD inputs (e.g. "café" composed
 74:   // vs decomposed) would otherwise yield different MATTR/lexicon hits.
 75:   const normalized = text ? text.normalize('NFC') : '';
 76:   const paragraphs = splitParagraphs(normalized);
 77: 
 78:   // Document-level leakage scan (issue #332). Near-proof-grade: a single hit is
 79:   // strong evidence of pasted model output, so it forces the document hot
 80:   // regardless of the per-paragraph stylometry/lexicon signals.
 81:   const markupLeakage = detectMarkupLeakage(normalized);
 82:   // Density-gated discourse tells (issue #334): fake-candor openers (>=2) and
 83:   // decorative thematic breaks (>=3). The density gate is document-level, but
 84:   // hot status is attributed to the paragraphs that carry a tell (issue #391,
 85:   // mirroring the playground's per-paragraph candorHot/thematicBreakHot), so
 86:   // flagged paragraphs enter rewrite scope and the hot ratio reflects them.
 87:   const discourseTells = detectDiscourseTells(normalized);
 88:   const paraCandorCounts = paragraphs.map((p) => detectFakeCandor(p).count);
 89:   const paraThematicBreakCounts = paragraphs.map((p) => detectThematicBreaks(p).count);
 90:   // ko translationese (번역투/calque) — lexical, NOT structural. Advisory signal:
 91:   // surfaced for callers/SKILL but deliberately NOT folded into `hot` (these
 92:   // constructions appear in good Korean too; gating hot would regress FP).
 93:   const translationese = detectTranslationese(normalized, { lang });
 94:   const koPostEditese = koreanPostEditeseFeatures(normalized, { lang });
 95:   const structuralClassifier = structuralModelVerdict(normalized, { lang, model: structuralModel });
 96:   const lexicon =
 97:     providedLexicon ??
 98:     (repoRoot ? loadLexicon(lang, repoRoot) : { strict: [], phrases: [] });
 99: 
100:   // §8 skip conditions are advisory only — production callers (SKILL.md 4.6/4.7)
101:   // can suppress meta-block emission, but the benchmark wants raw signals on
102:   // single-paragraph fixtures so we compute them unconditionally.
103:   const totalSentences = paragraphs.reduce(
104:     (n, p) => n + splitProseSentences(p).length,
105:     0
106:   );
107:   const skipReason =
108:     paragraphs.length <= 2 ? 'paragraphs<=2' :
109:     totalSentences <= 2 ? 'sentences<=2' :
110:     null;
111: 
112:   const analyzed = paragraphs.map((paragraph, idx) => {
113:     const sentences = splitProseSentences(paragraph);
114:     const sentenceTokens = sentences.map((sentence) => tokenize(sentence, { lang }));
115:     const sentenceTokenCounts = sentenceTokens.map((t) => t.length);
116:     const allTokens = sentenceTokens.flat();
117: 
118:     const cv = burstinessCV(sentenceTokenCounts);
119:     const cvBand =
120:       sentences.length >= minBurstinessSentences
121:         ? classifyBurstiness(cv, burstinessBands)
122:         : null;
123:     const mattrValue = mattr(allTokens, mattrWindow);
124:     const mattrBand = classifyMattr(mattrValue, mattrBands);
125:     const lex = computeDensity(paragraph, allTokens, lexicon);
126:     const koSignals = lang === 'ko'
127:       ? koreanDiagnostics(paragraph, sentences.length, {
128:           enabled: koDiagnosticsEnabled,
129:           bands: koDiagnosticBands,
130:         })
131:       : {};
132: 
133:     const endingMonotony = lang === 'ko' ? koreanEndingMonotony(sentences) : null;
134:     // KO uniform plain-다 register: low burstiness AND -다 dominance. Unlike the
135:     // standard burstiness trigger (cvBand) this does NOT require the 3-sentence
136:     // minimum, so it catches short AI Korean the band gate skips, while the -다
137:     // conjuncts keep formal/conversational human Korean out (DEFAULT_KO_ENDING_MONOTONY).
138:     const endingMonotonyHot = Boolean(
139:       endingMonotony &&
140:       allTokens.length >= koEndingMonotonyBands.minTokens &&
141:       cv != null &&
142:       cv < burstinessBands.low &&
143:       endingMonotony.daRatio != null &&
144:       endingMonotony.daRatio >= koEndingMonotonyBands.minDaRatio &&
145:       endingMonotony.daCount >= koEndingMonotonyBands.minDaCount
146:     );
147: 
148:     const lexiconHot = classifyLexiconHot(lex, {
149:       lang,
150:       densityThreshold: lexiconDensityThreshold,
151:       minHotMatches: lexiconMinHotMatches,
152:     });
153:     // Discourse-tell attribution (#391): this paragraph carries a tell AND the
154:     // document-level density gate fired (>=2 candor openers / >=3 breaks).
155:     const candorHot = discourseTells.fakeCandor.hot && paraCandorCounts[idx] >= 1;
156:     const thematicBreakHot = discourseTells.thematicBreaks.hot && paraThematicBreakCounts[idx] >= 1;
157:     const hot =
158:       cvBand === 'low' ||
159:       mattrBand === 'low' ||
160:       lexiconHot ||
161:       Boolean(koSignals.koDiagnostics?.hot) ||
162:       candorHot ||
163:       thematicBreakHot ||
164:       endingMonotonyHot;
165: 
166:     return {
167:       id: `P${idx + 1}`,
168:       sentenceCount: sentences.length,
169:       tokenCount: allTokens.length,
170:       burstiness: { cv, band: cvBand },
171:       mattr: { value: mattrValue, band: mattrBand },
172:       lexicon: { ...lex, hot: lexiconHot },
173:       ...koSignals,
174:       candorHot,
175:       candorCount: paraCandorCounts[idx],
176:       thematicBreakHot,
177:       thematicBreakCount: paraThematicBreakCounts[idx],
178:       endingMonotony,
179:       endingMonotonyHot,
180:       // Divider-only pseudo-paragraph (a bare `---` line between blank lines).
181:       // Hot attribution still applies (the divider itself is rewrite scope),
182:       // but prose gates use this to keep their ratios on actual prose.
183:       thematicBreakOnly: isThematicBreakOnly(paragraph),
184:       hot,
185:     };
186:   });
187: 
188:   return {
189:     lang,
190:     skipped: Boolean(skipReason),
191:     skipReason,
192:     paragraphs: analyzed,
193:     markupLeakage,
194:     discourseTells,
195:     translationese,
196:     koPostEditese,
197:     // No `discourseTells.hot` disjunct here: whenever the document-level density
198:     // gate fires, at least one paragraph carries the tell (candor regexes cannot
199:     // span paragraph breaks; thematic breaks are whole lines), so the
200:     // per-paragraph attribution above already makes some paragraph hot.
201:     hot: markupLeakage.leaked || structuralClassifier.hot === true || analyzed.some((p) => p.hot),
202:     structuralClassifier,
203:   };
204: }
205: 
206: export {
207:   splitParagraphs,
208:   splitSentences,
209:   splitProseSentences,
210:   tokenize,
211:   burstinessCV,
212:   mattr,
213:   classifyBurstiness,
214:   classifyMattr,
215:   classifyKoreanDiagnostics,
216:   koreanDiagnostics,
217:   commaDensity,
218:   koreanPosDiversityProxy,
219:   koreanSpacingFeatures,
220:   koreanPostEditeseFeatures,
221:   koreanEndingMonotony,
222:   detectKoreanRegister,
223:   loadLexicon,
224:   computeDensity,
225:   extractStructuralFeatures,
226:   structuralFeatureRecord,
227:   STRUCTURAL_FEATURE_NAMES,
228:   applyScaler,
229:   fitScaler,
230:   normalizeStructuralModel,
231:   predictStructuralScore,
232:   structuralModelVerdict,
233:   thresholdForMaxFpr,
234:   trainLogReg,
235:   loadStructuralModel,
236:   resolveStructuralModelPath,
237: };
````
