````javascript
  1: // Korean translationese (번역투 / calque) detector. The stylometry + lexicon
  2: // signals catch STRUCTURE (sentence rhythm, AI lexicon); they do NOT catch
  3: // lexical calques — phrasings that are grammatical Korean but read as
  4: // translated-from-English ("커맨드 기둥" for "command pillars", "~에 의해" passives,
  5: // "당신" for "you"). This deterministic, auditable detector fills that gap.
  6: //
  7: // IMPORTANT — precision first. Most of these constructions ALSO appear in good
  8: // native Korean (formal/technical prose especially). So this is a DENSITY-GATED
  9: // SUSPICION signal, not proof: a single "~에 의해" means nothing. It is surfaced
 10: // as its own `translationese` signal and does NOT flip the document `hot`
 11: // verdict (so it cannot regress benchmark false positives); the SKILL / callers
 12: // decide what to do with it. Each rule ships a before→after example.
 13: //
 14: // ko-only for now (calques are language-specific).
 15: import { splitProseSentences } from './segment.js';
 16: // The six shared interference rules are owned by catalog/ko-interference.js —
 17: // embed the catalog objects via getKoInterferenceRule(); never copy a pattern.
 18: import { getKoInterferenceRule } from './catalog/ko-interference.js';
 19: 
 20: // Strong rules are rarer and can satisfy the hot gate; weak rules are common
 21: // advisory evidence and never make the detector hot on their own.
 22: // Each rule: { id, label, strong, re() -> fresh global RegExp, minCount?, example:{before,after} }
 23: const RULES = [
 24:   {
 25:     id: 'noun-calque',
 26:     label: '직역 명사구 (pillar/layer 류 calque)',
 27:     strong: true,
 28:     re: () => /커맨드 기둥|명령(?:어)? 기둥|기둥 커맨드|[가-힣]{1,32} 레이어로서/g,
 29:     example: { before: '세 가지 커맨드 기둥을 설치합니다.', after: '핵심 커맨드 세 가지를 설치합니다.' },
 30:   },
 31:   {
 32:     id: 'dummy-subject',
 33:     label: '가주어 "그것은/이것은" (English "it is")',
 34:     strong: true,
 35:     re: () => /(?:^|[.!?。]\s+|\n)\s*(?:그것은|이것은|그것이|이것이)\s/g,
 36:     example: { before: '그것은 매우 중요하다.', after: '매우 중요하다.' },
 37:   },
 38:   {
 39:     id: 'direct-address-you',
 40:     label: '"당신" 직접 호칭 (English "you")',
 41:     strong: true,
 42:     // Hangul boundary guards (matching a16-pronoun-literal) keep 당신 out of
 43:     // unspaced compounds like 해당신청 / 사당신축 (#442).
 44:     re: () => /(?<![가-힣])당신(?:은|이|의|에게|을|를|께서|께)?(?![가-힣])/g,
 45:     example: { before: '당신은 이것을 설정할 수 있습니다.', after: '이건 설정할 수 있다.' },
 46:   },
 47:   getKoInterferenceRule('a16-pronoun-literal'),
 48:   getKoInterferenceRule('a19-double-particle'),
 49:   getKoInterferenceRule('passive-e-uihae'),
 50:   getKoInterferenceRule('t2-by-passive'),
 51:   getKoInterferenceRule('a8-double-passive'),
 52:   {
 53:     id: 'have-overuse',
 54:     label: '"~을 가지고 있다" (English "have")',
 55:     strong: false,
 56:     re: () => /(?:을|를)\s*(?:가지(?:고 있|고 있습니다|고 있다)|갖(?:고 있|고 있습니다|고 있다))/g,
 57:     example: { before: '이 도구는 유연성을 가지고 있습니다.', after: '이 도구는 유연합니다.' },
 58:   },
 59:   getKoInterferenceRule('a7-light-verb'),
 60:   {
 61:     id: 'one-of',
 62:     label: '"~중 하나" (English "one of the")',
 63:     strong: false,
 64:     re: () => /중\s*하나(?:이다|입니다|인|로|다|예요)?/g,
 65:     example: { before: '가장 빠른 도구 중 하나입니다.', after: '손꼽히게 빠릅니다.' },
 66:   },
 67:   {
 68:     id: 'provides',
 69:     label: '"~을 제공합니다" (English "provides")',
 70:     strong: false,
 71:     re: () => /(?:을|를)\s*제공(?:합니다|한다|해 줍니다|해준다)/g,
 72:     example: { before: '다양한 기능을 제공합니다.', after: '여러 기능을 쓸 수 있다.' },
 73:   },
 74:   {
 75:     id: 'as-follows',
 76:     label: '"다음과 같습니다" (English "as follows")',
 77:     strong: false,
 78:     re: () => /다음과\s*같(?:습니다|다|은|이)/g,
 79:     example: { before: '사용법은 다음과 같습니다.', after: '사용법은 이렇다.' },
 80:   },
 81:   {
 82:     id: 'make-easy',
 83:     label: '"~하게 만들어 준다" (English "make it ~")',
 84:     strong: false,
 85:     re: () => /(?:쉽게|가능하게|간단하게|편하게)\s*(?:만들어\s*(?:줍니다|준다|줘)|만듭니다|만든다)/g,
 86:     example: { before: '설치를 쉽게 만들어 줍니다.', after: '설치가 쉬워진다.' },
 87:   },
 88:   getKoInterferenceRule('c11-connective-comma'),
 89: ];
 90: 
 91: const ABS_MIN = 4;        // need at least this many independent evidence spans, and
 92: const DENSITY_MIN = 0.5;  // at least this many independent spans per prose sentence.
 93: const STRONG_MIN = 1;     // weak/common rules are advisory-only unless a strong signal also fires.
 94: 
 95: 
 96: /**
 97:  * Scan ko text for translationese (calque) markers.
 98:  * @param {string} text
 99:  * @param {{lang?: string}} [opts]
100:  * @returns {{count:number, density:number, sentences:number, byRule:Array, hits:string[], hot:boolean, thresholds:{count:number,density:number,strong:number}}}
101:  */
102: export function detectTranslationese(text, opts = {}) {
103:   const lang = opts.lang ?? 'ko';
104:   const str = typeof text === 'string' ? text : '';
105:   if (lang !== 'ko' || !str) {
106:     return { count: 0, density: 0, sentences: 0, byRule: [], hits: [], hot: false, thresholds: { count: ABS_MIN, density: DENSITY_MIN, strong: STRONG_MIN } };
107:   }
108:   const byRule = [];
109:   const hits = [];
110:   const evidence = [];
111:   for (const rule of RULES) {
112:     const matches = collectRuleMatches(str, rule);
113:     const minCount = rule.minCount ?? 1;
114:     if (matches.length >= minCount) {
115:       byRule.push({ id: rule.id, label: rule.label, strong: rule.strong, count: matches.length, example: rule.example });
116:       hits.push(...new Set(matches.map((m) => m.text.trim()).filter(Boolean)));
117:       evidence.push(...matches.map((match) => ({ ...match, strong: Boolean(rule.strong) })));
118:     }
119:   }
120:   const independentEvidence = selectIndependentEvidence(evidence);
121:   const count = independentEvidence.length;
122:   const strongCount = independentEvidence.filter((match) => match.strong).length;
123:   const sentences = Math.max(1, splitProseSentences(str).length);
124:   const density = count / sentences;
125:   // Conservative: needs enough independent spans, enough density, and at least
126:   // one strong signal. Weak/common calques remain advisory even when clustered.
127:   const hot = count >= ABS_MIN && density >= DENSITY_MIN && strongCount >= STRONG_MIN;
128:   return {
129:     count,
130:     density: Number(density.toFixed(3)),
131:     sentences,
132:     byRule: byRule.sort((a, b) => b.count - a.count),
133:     hits: [...new Set(hits)].slice(0, 8),
134:     hot,
135:     thresholds: { count: ABS_MIN, density: DENSITY_MIN, strong: STRONG_MIN },
136:   };
137: }
138: 
139: function collectRuleMatches(str, rule) {
140:   const re = rule.re();
141:   const matches = [];
142:   let match;
143:   while ((match = re.exec(str)) !== null) {
144:     matches.push({
145:       text: match[0],
146:       start: match.index,
147:       end: match.index + match[0].length,
148:     });
149:     if (match[0] === '') re.lastIndex += 1;
150:   }
151:   return matches;
152: }
153: 
154: function selectIndependentEvidence(matches) {
155:   const ranked = [...matches].sort((a, b) => {
156:     if (a.strong !== b.strong) return a.strong ? -1 : 1;
157:     const lengthDelta = (b.end - b.start) - (a.end - a.start);
158:     if (lengthDelta !== 0) return lengthDelta;
159:     return a.start - b.start;
160:   });
161: 
162:   // Greedy non-overlapping selection in priority order. A `selected.some(...)`
163:   // overlap scan is O(n²) on attacker-influenceable input (~160ms at ~6000
164:   // matches, #508 G6). Mark covered character positions in a bitmap instead so
165:   // each overlap check/mark is O(span length) — linear in total text, not in
166:   // match count — while preserving the exact half-open-interval semantics.
167:   let maxEnd = 0;
168:   for (const m of matches) if (m.end > maxEnd) maxEnd = m.end;
169:   const covered = new Uint8Array(maxEnd);
170:   const selected = [];
171: 
172:   for (const match of ranked) {
173:     let overlap = false;
174:     for (let i = match.start; i < match.end; i++) {
175:       if (covered[i]) { overlap = true; break; }
176:     }
177:     if (overlap) continue;
178:     for (let i = match.start; i < match.end; i++) covered[i] = 1;
179:     selected.push(match);
180:   }
181: 
182:   return selected.sort((a, b) => a.start - b.start);
183: }
184: 
185: export { RULES as TRANSLATIONESE_RULES, ABS_MIN, DENSITY_MIN, STRONG_MIN };
````

## File: tests/quality/benchmark.mjs
````javascript
  1: #!/usr/bin/env node
  2: // Quality benchmark for the deterministic stylometry layer.
  3: //
  4: // Iterates every fixture under tests/fixtures/suspect-zones/{lang}/{class}/*.md,
  5: // runs the in-tree analyzer (no LLM), and compares the predicted hot/cold
  6: // decision against the fixture's expected_hot label. Emits a per-language
  7: // confusion matrix + accuracy and writes the full per-fixture log to
  8: // tests/quality/results.json.
  9: //
 10: // Usage: node tests/quality/benchmark.mjs [--quiet]
 11: 
 12: import { readFileSync, readdirSync, writeFileSync, statSync } from 'node:fs';
 13: import { resolve, dirname } from 'node:path';
 14: import { fileURLToPath } from 'node:url';
 15: import yaml from 'js-yaml';
 16: 
 17: import { analyzeText } from '../../src/features/index.js';
 18: import { loadLexicon } from '../../src/features/lexicon.js';
 19: import { summarizeSignalStrength } from '../../src/features/signal-strength.js';
 20: import { summarizeRanking } from './ranking-metrics.mjs';
 21: import { summarizeSlices, lengthBucket } from './slice-metrics.mjs';
 22: import { resolveSliceFields } from './slice-metadata.mjs';
 23: 
 24: const __dirname = dirname(fileURLToPath(import.meta.url));
 25: const REPO_ROOT = resolve(__dirname, '../..');
 26: const FIXTURES_ROOT = resolve(REPO_ROOT, 'tests/fixtures/suspect-zones');
 27: const RESULTS_PATH = resolve(__dirname, 'results.json');
 28: const EXPECTED_RANGES_PATH = resolve(FIXTURES_ROOT, 'expected-ranges.json');
 29: const FIXTURE_SCHEMA_VERSION = 1;
 30: 
 31: const FRONTMATTER_RE = /^---\n([\s\S]*?)\n---\s*\n([\s\S]*)$/;
 32: 
 33: function parseFixture(path) {
 34:   const raw = readFileSync(path, 'utf8');
 35:   const m = raw.match(FRONTMATTER_RE);
 36:   if (!m) throw new Error(`Missing frontmatter: ${path}`);
 37:   return { meta: yaml.load(m[1]), body: m[2].trim(), path };
 38: }
 39: 
 40: function listFixtures() {
 41:   const out = [];
 42:   for (const lang of readdirSync(FIXTURES_ROOT)) {
 43:     const langDir = resolve(FIXTURES_ROOT, lang);
 44:     if (!statSync(langDir).isDirectory()) continue;
 45:     for (const cls of readdirSync(langDir)) {
 46:       const clsDir = resolve(langDir, cls);
 47:       if (!statSync(clsDir).isDirectory()) continue;
 48:       for (const file of readdirSync(clsDir)) {
 49:         if (!file.endsWith('.md')) continue;
 50:         out.push(resolve(clsDir, file));
 51:       }
 52:     }
 53:   }
 54:   return out.sort();
 55: }
 56: 
 57: function loadExpectedRanges() {
 58:   const raw = readFileSync(EXPECTED_RANGES_PATH, 'utf8');
 59:   const ranges = JSON.parse(raw);
 60:   if (ranges?.schemaVersion !== 1 || !ranges?.metrics || typeof ranges.metrics !== 'object') {
 61:     throw new Error(`${EXPECTED_RANGES_PATH}: expected schemaVersion=1 and metrics object`);
 62:   }
 63:   return ranges.metrics;
 64: }
 65: 
 66: function emptyMetrics() {
 67:   return { tp: 0, fp: 0, fn: 0, tn: 0, total: 0 };
 68: }
 69: 
 70: function updateMetrics(m, predicted, expected) {
 71:   m.total++;
 72:   if (predicted && expected) m.tp++;
 73:   else if (predicted && !expected) m.fp++;
 74:   else if (!predicted && expected) m.fn++;
 75:   else m.tn++;
 76: }
 77: 
 78: function summarize(m) {
 79:   const accuracy = m.total ? (m.tp + m.tn) / m.total : 0;
 80:   const precision = m.tp + m.fp ? m.tp / (m.tp + m.fp) : 0;
 81:   const recall = m.tp + m.fn ? m.tp / (m.tp + m.fn) : 0;
 82:   const f1 = precision + recall ? (2 * precision * recall) / (precision + recall) : 0;
 83:   return {
 84:     ...m,
 85:     accuracy: round(accuracy),
 86:     precision: round(precision),
 87:     recall: round(recall),
 88:     f1: round(f1),
 89:     n: m.total,
 90:     ci_low: round(wilsonInterval(m.tp + m.tn, m.total).low),
 91:     ci_high: round(wilsonInterval(m.tp + m.tn, m.total).high),
 92:     confidence_method: 'Wilson score interval, 95%',
 93:   };
 94: }
 95: 
 96: function rankingRecords(fixtures) {
 97:   return fixtures.map((fixture) => ({
 98:     score: fixture.signal_score,
 99:     expected: fixture.expected_hot,
100:   }));
101: }
102: 
103: function summarizeRankingByLanguage(fixtures) {
104:   const byLanguage = {};
105:   for (const fixture of fixtures) {
106:     byLanguage[fixture.lang] ||= [];
107:     byLanguage[fixture.lang].push({
108:       score: fixture.signal_score,
109:       expected: fixture.expected_hot,
110:     });
111:   }
112:   return Object.fromEntries(
113:     Object.entries(byLanguage)
114:       .sort(([a], [b]) => a.localeCompare(b))
115:       .map(([lang, records]) => [lang, summarizeRanking(records)])
116:   );
117: }
118: 
119: function round(n, digits = 3) {
120:   return Math.round(n * 10 ** digits) / 10 ** digits;
121: }
122: 
123: function wilsonInterval(successes, n, z = 1.959963984540054) {
124:   if (!n) return { low: 0, high: 0 };
125:   const phat = successes / n;
126:   const denom = 1 + (z ** 2) / n;
127:   const center = (phat + (z ** 2) / (2 * n)) / denom;
128:   const margin = (z * Math.sqrt((phat * (1 - phat) + (z ** 2) / (4 * n)) / n)) / denom;
129:   return {
130:     low: Math.max(0, center - margin),
131:     high: Math.min(1, center + margin),
132:   };
133: }
134: 
135: function detectorHot(result) {
136:   return {
137:     burstiness: result.paragraphs.some((p) => p.burstiness?.band === 'low'),
138:     koDiagnostics: result.paragraphs.some((p) => p.koDiagnostics?.hot),
139:     mattr: result.paragraphs.some((p) => p.mattr?.band === 'low'),
140:     lexicon: result.paragraphs.some((p) => p.lexicon?.hot),
141:     endingMonotony: result.paragraphs.some((p) => p.endingMonotonyHot),
142:     candor: result.paragraphs.some((p) => p.candorHot),
143:     thematicBreak: result.paragraphs.some((p) => p.thematicBreakHot),
144:   };
145: }
146: 
147: function emptyDetectorMetrics() {
148:   return Object.fromEntries(
149:     Object.keys(detectorHot({ paragraphs: [] })).map((name) => [name, emptyMetrics()])
150:   );
151: }
152: 
153: function validateExpectedMetrics(path, expected = {}, observed = {}) {
154:   const failures = [];
155:   if (expected.cv_band && observed.cv_band !== expected.cv_band) {
156:     failures.push(`cv_band expected ${expected.cv_band}, got ${observed.cv_band}`);
157:   }
158:   if (expected.mattr_band && observed.mattr_band !== expected.mattr_band) {
159:     failures.push(`mattr_band expected ${expected.mattr_band}, got ${observed.mattr_band}`);
160:   }
161:   if (typeof expected.lexicon_density_min === 'number' && observed.lexicon_density < expected.lexicon_density_min) {
162:     failures.push(`lexicon_density expected >= ${expected.lexicon_density_min}, got ${observed.lexicon_density}`);
163:   }
164:   if (typeof expected.lexicon_density_max === 'number' && observed.lexicon_density > expected.lexicon_density_max) {
165:     failures.push(`lexicon_density expected <= ${expected.lexicon_density_max}, got ${observed.lexicon_density}`);
166:   }
167:   if (Array.isArray(expected.cv_range) && !inRange(observed.cv, expected.cv_range)) {
168:     failures.push(`cv expected ${formatRange(expected.cv_range)}, got ${observed.cv}`);
169:   }
170:   if (Array.isArray(expected.mattr_range) && !inRange(observed.mattr, expected.mattr_range)) {
171:     failures.push(`mattr expected ${formatRange(expected.mattr_range)}, got ${observed.mattr}`);
172:   }
173:   if (Array.isArray(expected.lexicon_density_range) && !inRange(observed.lexicon_density, expected.lexicon_density_range)) {
174:     failures.push(`lexicon_density expected ${formatRange(expected.lexicon_density_range)}, got ${observed.lexicon_density}`);
175:   }
176:   if (expected.detectors) {
177:     for (const [name, expectedHot] of Object.entries(expected.detectors)) {
178:       if (observed.detectors?.[name] !== expectedHot) {
179:         failures.push(`detector.${name} expected ${expectedHot}, got ${observed.detectors?.[name]}`);
180:       }
181:     }
182:   }
183:   if (typeof expected.predicted_hot === 'boolean' && observed.predicted_hot !== expected.predicted_hot) {
184:     failures.push(`predicted_hot expected ${expected.predicted_hot}, got ${observed.predicted_hot}`);
185:   }
186:   // Pins per-paragraph attribution (#391): a document-level OR could keep
187:   // predicted_hot true while leaving every paragraph cold, which this catches.
188:   if (typeof expected.hot_paragraphs === 'number' && observed.hot_paragraphs !== expected.hot_paragraphs) {
189:     failures.push(`hot_paragraphs expected ${expected.hot_paragraphs}, got ${observed.hot_paragraphs}`);
190:   }
191:   // A hot fixture must stay visible to the signal-score ranking leg.
192:   if (typeof expected.signal_score_min === 'number' && observed.signal_score < expected.signal_score_min) {
193:     failures.push(`signal_score expected >= ${expected.signal_score_min}, got ${observed.signal_score}`);
194:   }
195:   if (failures.length) {
196:     throw new Error(`${path}: expected_metrics regression failed: ${failures.join('; ')}`);
197:   }
198: }
199: 
200: function inRange(value, [min, max]) {
201:   return typeof value === 'number' && value >= min && value <= max;
202: }
203: 
204: function formatRange([min, max]) {
205:   return `[${min}, ${max}]`;
206: }
207: 
208: function main() {
209:   const quiet = process.argv.includes('--quiet');
210:   const fixtures = listFixtures();
211:   const expectedRanges = loadExpectedRanges();
212: 
213:   if (fixtures.length === 0) {
214:     console.error('No fixtures found under', FIXTURES_ROOT);
215:     process.exit(2);
216:   }
217: 
218:   const lexicons = {};
219:   const perLanguage = {};
220:   const byDetector = {};
221:   const fixtureLog = [];
222: 
223:   for (const path of fixtures) {
224:     const { meta, body } = parseFixture(path);
225:     const lang = meta.language;
226:     if (typeof meta.expected_hot !== 'boolean') {
227:       throw new Error(
228:         `${path}: \`expected_hot\` must be a literal boolean (got ${typeof meta.expected_hot}: ${JSON.stringify(meta.expected_hot)})`
229:       );
230:     }
231:     if (!lexicons[lang]) lexicons[lang] = loadLexicon(lang, REPO_ROOT);
232:     if (!perLanguage[lang]) perLanguage[lang] = emptyMetrics();
233:     if (!byDetector[lang]) byDetector[lang] = emptyDetectorMetrics();
234: 
235:     const result = analyzeText(body, {
236:       lang,
237:       lexicon: lexicons[lang],
238:     });
239:     const predicted = result.hot;
240:     const expected = meta.expected_hot;
241:     updateMetrics(perLanguage[lang], predicted, expected);
242:     const detectors = detectorHot(result);
243:     for (const [name, hot] of Object.entries(detectors)) {
244:       updateMetrics(byDetector[lang][name], hot, expected);
245:     }
246: 
247:     const p = result.paragraphs[0] || {};
248:     const observed = {
249:       cv: round(p.burstiness?.cv ?? 0),
250:       cv_band: p.burstiness?.band,
251:       mattr: round(p.mattr?.value ?? 0),
252:       mattr_band: p.mattr?.band,
253:       lexicon_density: round(p.lexicon?.density ?? 0),
254:       lexicon_hits: p.lexicon?.hits ?? [],
255:       ko_diagnostics_hot: Boolean(p.koDiagnostics?.hot),
256:       ko_diagnostics_reasons: p.koDiagnostics?.reasons ?? [],
257:       ko_diagnostics_strength: round(p.koDiagnostics?.strength ?? 0),
258:       signal_score: round(summarizeSignalStrength(result.paragraphs)),
259:       hot_paragraphs: result.paragraphs.filter((x) => x.hot).length,
260:     };
261:     const pinned = expectedRanges[meta.fixture_id];
262:     if (!pinned) {
263:       throw new Error(
264:         `${path}: missing benchmark regression range. Run node scripts/update-benchmark-ranges.mjs after reviewing the fixture.`
265:       );
266:     }
267:     observed.detectors = detectors;
268:     observed.predicted_hot = predicted;
269:     validateExpectedMetrics(path, pinned, observed);
270:     if (meta.expected_metrics) validateExpectedMetrics(path, meta.expected_metrics, observed);
271:     fixtureLog.push({
272:       fixture_id: meta.fixture_id,
273:       lang,
274:       class: meta.class,
275:       expected_hot: expected,
276:       predicted_hot: predicted,
277:       correct: predicted === expected,
278:       detectors,
279:       ...observed,
280:       expected_metrics: meta.expected_metrics ?? null,
281:       // Slice dimensions (B2, report-only). language/class/length_bucket are
282:       // always derivable; the rest default to `unspecified` until the corpus
283:       // carries that metadata.
284:       length_bucket: lengthBucket([...body].length),
285:       // generator/edited resolved via the tested B2 reconciliation mapper
286:       // (Wave 0.1): explicit B2-native fields win; model_family/edit_depth
287:       // aliases and class defaults fill the rest. register/domain pass through.
288:       ...resolveSliceFields(meta),
289:     });
290:   }
291: 
292:   const summary = {};
293:   let totalCorrect = 0;
294:   let totalCount = 0;
295:   for (const [lang, m] of Object.entries(perLanguage)) {
296:     summary[lang] = summarize(m);
297:     summary[lang].byDetector = Object.fromEntries(
298:       Object.entries(byDetector[lang]).map(([name, metrics]) => [name, summarize(metrics)])
299:     );
300:     totalCorrect += m.tp + m.tn;
301:     totalCount += m.total;
302:   }
303:   const overallAccuracy = totalCount ? totalCorrect / totalCount : 0;
304:   const overallCi = wilsonInterval(totalCorrect, totalCount);
305: 
306:   const results = {
307:     schemaVersion: 3,
308:     fixtureSchemaVersion: FIXTURE_SCHEMA_VERSION,
309:     nodeVersion: process.version,
310:     generatedAt: new Date().toISOString(),
311:     fixtureCount: fixtureLog.length,
312:     overallAccuracy: round(overallAccuracy),
313:     overall: {
314:       accuracy: round(overallAccuracy),
315:       n: totalCount,
316:       ci_low: round(overallCi.low),
317:       ci_high: round(overallCi.high),
318:       confidence_method: 'Wilson score interval, 95%',
319:     },
320:     perLanguage: summary,
321:     ranking: {
322:       note: 'Signal-score ranking over the checked-in fixture corpus; diagnostic only, not a public generalization claim.',
323:       score: 'signal_score from the strongest deterministic paragraph trigger, averaged per fixture',
324:       overall: summarizeRanking(rankingRecords(fixtureLog)),
325:       perLanguage: summarizeRankingByLanguage(fixtureLog),
326:     },
327:     slices: summarizeSlices(
328:       fixtureLog.map((f) => ({
329:         language: f.lang,
330:         class: f.class,
331:         lengthBucket: f.length_bucket,
332:         domain: f.domain,
333:         register: f.register,
334:         generator: f.generator,
335:         edited: f.edited,
336:         predicted_hot: f.predicted_hot,
337:         expected_hot: f.expected_hot,
338:       }))
339:     ),
340:     fixtures: fixtureLog,
341:   };
342: 
343:   writeFileSync(RESULTS_PATH, JSON.stringify(results, null, 2) + '\n');
344: 
345:   const wrong = fixtureLog.filter((f) => !f.correct);
346: 
347:   if (!quiet) {
348:     console.log(`# Quality benchmark — ${fixtureLog.length} fixtures`);
349:     console.log(`Overall accuracy: ${(overallAccuracy * 100).toFixed(1)}%`);
350:     console.log(`Signal ROC-AUC: ${results.ranking.overall.roc_auc.toFixed(3)} · PR-AUC: ${results.ranking.overall.pr_auc.toFixed(3)} · best-F1 threshold: ${results.ranking.overall.bestF1.threshold}`);
351:     console.log();
352:     console.log('| lang | n | accuracy | precision | recall | f1 | TP | FP | FN | TN |');
353:     console.log('|------|---|----------|-----------|--------|----|----|----|----|----|');
354:     for (const [lang, s] of Object.entries(summary)) {
355:       console.log(
356:         `| ${lang} | ${s.total} | ${(s.accuracy * 100).toFixed(1)}% | ${(s.precision * 100).toFixed(1)}% | ${(s.recall * 100).toFixed(1)}% | ${s.f1.toFixed(2)} | ${s.tp} | ${s.fp} | ${s.fn} | ${s.tn} |`
357:       );
358:     }
359:     console.log();
360:     console.log('| lang | detector | n | accuracy | precision | recall | f1 | TP | FP | FN | TN |');
361:     console.log('|------|----------|---|----------|-----------|--------|----|----|----|----|----|');
362:     for (const [lang, s] of Object.entries(summary)) {
363:       for (const [detector, d] of Object.entries(s.byDetector)) {
364:         console.log(
365:           `| ${lang} | ${detector} | ${d.total} | ${(d.accuracy * 100).toFixed(1)}% | ${(d.precision * 100).toFixed(1)}% | ${(d.recall * 100).toFixed(1)}% | ${d.f1.toFixed(2)} | ${d.tp} | ${d.fp} | ${d.fn} | ${d.tn} |`
366:         );
367:       }
368:     }
369:     console.log();
370:     if (wrong.length > 0) {
371:       console.log(`Misclassified (${wrong.length}):`);
372:       for (const f of wrong) {
373:         console.log(
374:           `  ${f.fixture_id} (${f.class}) → predicted=${f.predicted_hot}, expected=${f.expected_hot} | cv=${f.cv} ${f.cv_band}, mattr=${f.mattr} ${f.mattr_band}, lex=${f.lexicon_density}/1000`
375:         );
376:       }
377:     } else {
378:       console.log('All fixtures classified correctly.');
379:     }
380:     console.log(`\nFull log: ${RESULTS_PATH}`);
381:   }
382: 
383:   // Non-zero exit on any misclassification so CI catches regressions even in --quiet mode.
384:   if (wrong.length > 0) process.exitCode = 1;
385: }
386: 
387: main();
````

## File: core/scoring.md
````markdown
  1: ---
  2: name: AI-Likeness Scoring Algorithm
  3: version: 1.0.0
  4: description: Pattern-based AI-likeness scoring reference for patina score and ouroboros modes
  5: ---
  6: 
  7: # AI-Likeness Scoring Algorithm
  8: 
  9: Pattern-based scoring that converts AI pattern detection results into a numeric 0-100 score.
 10: Used by `--score` mode and `--ouroboros` loop for termination gating.
 11: 
 12: ---
 13: 
 14: ## 1. Severity Scale (Per-Detection)
 15: 
 16: Severity is assigned **per detection** by the LLM during the pattern scan phase.
 17: It is NOT intrinsic to the pattern — the same pattern may receive different severity
 18: depending on how egregiously it appears in context.
 19: 
 20: | Level | Points | Criteria |
 21: |-------|--------|----------|
 22: | High | 3 | Pattern is pervasive — appears multiple times or is especially blatant |
 23: | Medium | 2 | Pattern present at moderate frequency or impact |
 24: | Low | 1 | Pattern barely present — isolated occurrence |
 25: | Not detected | 0 | Pattern not found in text |
 26: 
 27: ---
 28: 
 29: ## 2. Severity Assignment Rubric
 30: 
 31: To reduce variance between runs, follow these guidelines when assigning severity:
 32: 
 33: ### General Rubric (4+ paragraph text)
 34: 
 35: | Instances | Severity |
 36: |-----------|----------|
 37: | 1-2 isolated occurrences | Low (1) |
 38: | 3-5 occurrences, or concentrated in one section | Medium (2) |
 39: | 6+ occurrences, or pervasive throughout | High (3) |
 40: 
 41: ### Special Cases
 42: 
 43: - **Structure patterns (#25-28):** Assess at document level, not instance count.
 44:   A single structural issue (e.g., every paragraph follows identical template) is High.
 45: - **Communication patterns (#19-21):** A single clear chatbot expression
 46:   (e.g., "좋은 질문입니다!") may be High — these are strong AI signals regardless of count.
 47: - **Short text (1-2 paragraphs):** Adjust thresholds proportionally.
 48:   2 instances in 2 paragraphs = Medium, not Low.
 49: 
 50: ---
 51: 
 52: ## 3. Category Derivation
 53: 
 54: Categories are derived **dynamically** from pack frontmatter, not hardcoded.
 55: 
 56: ```
 57: Category = pack frontmatter `pack` field minus language prefix
 58: Example: pack: ko-content  → category: content
 59:          pack: en-style    → category: style
 60:          pack: ko-custom   → category: custom
 61: 
 62: Pattern count = pack frontmatter `patterns` field
 63: ```
 64: 
 65: If multiple packs map to the same category (e.g., `ko-content` and `custom/ko-content`),
 66: their patterns are merged and counts summed for that category.
 67: 
 68: Unknown categories (from custom packs not in the weight config) get default weight: **0.10**.
 69: 
 70: ---
 71: 
 72: ## 4. Category Weights
 73: 
 74: ### Korean (ko)
 75: 
 76: | Category | Weight | Patterns | Notes |
 77: |----------|--------|----------|-------|
 78: | content | 0.18 | 6 | |
 79: | language | 0.18 | 9 | |
 80: | style | 0.18 | 6 | |
 81: | communication | 0.13 | 4 | |
 82: | filler | 0.08 | 4 | |
 83: | structure | 0.15 | 5 | |
 84: | viral-hook | 0.10 | 9 | score-only (no rewrite) |
 85: | **Total** | **1.00** | **43** | |
 86: 
 87: ### English (en)
 88: 
 89: | Category | Weight | Patterns | Notes |
 90: |----------|--------|----------|-------|
 91: | content | 0.20 | 6 | |
 92: | language | 0.20 | 9 | |
 93: | style | 0.20 | 6 | |
 94: | communication | 0.12 | 4 | |
 95: | filler | 0.08 | 4 | |
 96: | structure | 0.10 | 5 | |
 97: | viral-hook | 0.10 | 9 | score-only (no rewrite) |
 98: | **Total** | **1.00** | **43** | |
 99: 
100: ### Chinese (zh)
101: 
102: | Category | Weight | Patterns | Notes |
103: |----------|--------|----------|-------|
104: | content | 0.18 | 6 | |
105: | language | 0.18 | 9 | |
106: | style | 0.18 | 6 | |
107: | communication | 0.13 | 4 | |
108: | filler | 0.08 | 4 | |
109: | structure | 0.15 | 5 | |
110: | viral-hook | 0.10 | 9 | score-only (no rewrite) |
111: | **Total** | **1.00** | **43** | |
112: 
113: ### Japanese (ja)
114: 
115: | Category | Weight | Patterns | Notes |
116: |----------|--------|----------|-------|
117: | content | 0.18 | 6 | |
118: | language | 0.18 | 9 | |
119: | style | 0.18 | 6 | |
120: | communication | 0.13 | 4 | |
121: | filler | 0.08 | 4 | |
122: | structure | 0.15 | 5 | |
123: | viral-hook | 0.10 | 9 | score-only (no rewrite) |
124: | **Total** | **1.00** | **43** | |
125: 
126: Weights are configurable via `ouroboros.category-weights.{lang}` in `.patina.yaml`.
127: 
128: **viral-hook weight review (issue #154):** kept at `0.10`. The pack remains score-only and the three added patterns improve the category denominator/granularity rather than broadening it enough to justify a larger score contribution. Rewrite, diff, and Ouroboros modes still skip the pack.
129: 
130: ---
131: 
132: ## 5. Profile Override Adjustments
133: 
134: Before summing severities, apply profile `pattern-overrides` modifiers:
135: 
136: | Override | Factor | Effect |
137: |----------|--------|--------|
138: | amplify | × 1.5 (cap at 3) | Increases severity contribution |
139: | reduce | × 0.5 | Decreases severity contribution |
140: | suppress | × 0.0 | Excludes pattern entirely |
141: | normal (default) | × 1.0 | No change |
142: 
143: Example: blog profile suppresses #14 (bold) → pattern #14 severity becomes 0,
144: excluded from ko-style category calculation.
145: 
146: ### Language-Scoped Overrides
147: 
148: `pattern-overrides` may be nested under a language code (`ko:`, `en:`) to avoid
149: cross-language number collisions (e.g., ko #8 is "~적 접미사" while en #8 is
150: "Copula Avoidance" — the same number refers to unrelated patterns in each language).
151: 
152: ```yaml
153: # Language-scoped format (recommended for multi-language profiles)
154: pattern-overrides:
155:   ko:
156:     8: amplify    # ko-language #8 (~적 접미사)
157:     14: suppress  # ko-style #14 (볼드체)
158:   en:
159:     8: amplify    # en-language #8 (Copula Avoidance)
160:     14: suppress  # en-style #14 (Boldface)
161: ```
162: 
163: **Resolution rule:** When the active language has a sub-section under `pattern-overrides`,
164: apply **only** that sub-section's overrides. Top-level (unscoped) overrides apply to all
165: languages and are merged before language-scoped ones (language-scoped wins on conflict).
166: 
167: ---
168: 
169: ## 6. Scoring Formula
170: 
171: ### Per-Category Score
172: 
173: ```
174: category_score = (sum of adjusted severities / (pattern_count × 3)) × 100
175: ```
176: 
177: - `sum of adjusted severities`: sum severity points for all detected patterns in category,
178:   after applying profile override factors
179: - `pattern_count × 3`: maximum possible score (all patterns detected at High severity)
180: - Result: 0-100 per category
181: 
182: ### Overall Score
183: 
184: ```
185: overall_score = Σ(category_score × category_weight) for all categories
186: ```
187: 
188: ### Worked Example (Korean, default profile)
189: 
190: Input text detected patterns:
191: 
192: | Pattern | Category | Raw Severity | Override | Adjusted |
193: |---------|----------|-------------|----------|----------|
194: | #1 과도한 중요성 부여 | content | High (3) | normal | 3 |
195: | #3 피상적 분석 | content | Medium (2) | normal | 2 |
196: | #5 모호한 출처 | content | Low (1) | normal | 1 |
197: | #8 ~적 접미사 | language | Medium (2) | normal | 2 |
198: | #14 볼드체 | style | High (3) | normal | 3 |
199: | #17 이모지 | style | Medium (2) | normal | 2 |
200: | #22 채움 표현 | filler | Low (1) | normal | 1 |
201: | #25 구조적 반복 | structure | High (3) | normal | 3 |
202: 
203: Category scores:
204: 
205: | Category | Detected | Sum | Max (count×3) | Score |
206: |----------|----------|-----|---------------|-------|
207: | content | 3/6 | 3+2+1=6 | 6×3=18 | 33.3 |
208: | language | 1/9 | 2 | 9×3=27 | 7.4 |
209: | style | 2/6 | 3+2=5 | 6×3=18 | 27.8 |
210: | communication | 0/4 | 0 | 4×3=12 | 0.0 |
211: | filler | 1/4 | 1 | 4×3=12 | 8.3 |
212: | structure | 1/5 | 3 | 5×3=15 | 20.0 |
213: | viral-hook | 0/9 | 0 | 9×3=27 | 0.0 |
214: 
215: Overall = 33.3×0.18 + 7.4×0.18 + 27.8×0.18 + 0.0×0.13 + 8.3×0.08 + 20.0×0.15 + 0.0×0.10
216:        = 6.0 + 1.3 + 5.0 + 0.0 + 0.7 + 3.0 + 0.0
217:        = **16.0**
218: 
219: (Weighted terms are computed from the unrounded category scores and shown to
220: one decimal; they sum exactly to the displayed total. Unrounded: 6.000 + 1.333
221: + 5.000 + 0.000 + 0.667 + 3.000 = 16.000 → 16.0.)
222: 
223: Interpretation: 16-30 range = "거의 사람다움" (Mostly human, minor traces)
224: 
225: ---
226: 
227: ## 7. Score Interpretation
228: 
229: | Range | Label | Meaning |
230: |-------|-------|---------|
231: | 0-15 | 사람다움 | Strongly human-like |
232: | 16-30 | 거의 사람다움 | Mostly human, minor AI traces |
233: | 31-50 | 혼재 | Mixed signals, noticeable AI patterns |
234: | 51-70 | AI 느낌 | Clearly AI-generated |
235: | 71-100 | AI 생성 | Heavily AI-generated |
236: 
237: > **Variance Note:** Scores have expected variance of **±8-10 points** between runs
238: > due to LLM severity assignment. Use score ranges, not exact numbers, for comparison.
239: > A score of 42 should be interpreted as "roughly 32-52 range" for decision-making.
240: 
241: ---
242: 
243: ## 8. Known Limitations
244: 
245: - **Custom pattern packs** are auto-discovered and scored. A new category
246:   (e.g., `custom/patterns/ko-domain.md` with `pack: ko-domain`) gets default weight 0.10.
247: - **LLM non-determinism** means the same text may score differently across runs.
248:   The formula is deterministic; the severity assignment is not.
249: - **Fidelity scoring** (meaning preservation vs original) is defined in §§ 9–13 below
250:   and integrated into `--score` and `--ouroboros` pipelines.
251: 
252: ### Short-text boost (v3.11 Phase 3.2)
253: 
254: For inputs ≤200 non-whitespace chars OR ≤3 non-empty paragraphs, register-
255: sensitive categories get a 1.5x severity multiplier (capped at 3 per detection):
256: 
257: - `language` — 종결어미, register cues, sentence form patterns
258: - `style` — connectors, transition fillers, formulaic openers
259: - `viral-hook` — shock numbers, clickbait closes, hyperbolic lexicon
260: 
261: Rationale: case-04 found that voice/register shifts (e.g., `~다` ↔ `~습니다`)
262: are clearly perceived by humans but barely move the standard formula because
263: short texts only accumulate 1–2 pattern detections. The boost surfaces those
264: shifts so single-paragraph score deltas align with reader intuition.
265: 
266: The boost is applied at severity-assignment time by the scoring LLM (per the
267: prompt instruction in `buildScoreInstructions`), not as a post-multiplier on
268: category scores. This keeps the formula in §6 unchanged.
269: 
270: ---
271: 
272: ## 9. Fidelity Scoring — Overview
273: 
274: AI-likeness (§§ 1–7) measures *how AI-like the output sounds*.
275: Fidelity measures *how faithfully the output preserves the original meaning*.
276: 
277: Both dimensions are necessary: aggressive humanization can achieve a low AI score
278: by deleting content or changing meaning entirely. Fidelity scoring guards against this.
279: 
280: Fidelity scoring is integrated into `--score` and `--ouroboros` pipelines.
281: See SKILL.md § 6 (score mode) for integration details.
282: 
283: ---
284: 
285: ## 10. Fidelity Criteria
286: 
287: Four criteria, each scored independently by the LLM comparing original → output:
288: 
289: ### 10.1 Claims Preserved
290: 
291: Every factual claim in the original appears (perhaps rephrased) in the output.
292: 
293: | Level | Points | Criteria |
294: |-------|--------|----------|
295: | High | 3 | All key claims preserved — no factual content lost |
296: | Medium | 2 | Minor claims omitted — supporting details or examples dropped, but core argument intact |
297: | Low | 1 | Significant claims missing — one or more central facts or arguments absent |
298: | Fail | 0 | Core meaning lost — the output says something fundamentally different |
299: 
300: ### 10.2 No Fabrication
301: 
302: The output does not add claims, facts, or specifics not present or implied by the original.
303: 
304: | Level | Points | Criteria |
305: |-------|--------|----------|
306: | High | 3 | No fabrication — every claim in the output traces to the original |
307: | Medium | 2 | Minor additions — a reasonable inference stated as fact, or an illustrative example added |
308: | Low | 1 | Noticeable fabrication — specific numbers, names, or claims not in the original |
309: | Fail | 0 | Significant fabrication — output contains substantial invented content |
310: 
311: ### 10.3 Tone Match
312: 
313: The output's register matches the original (or the profile's target register, if explicitly overridden).
314: 
315: | Level | Points | Criteria |
316: |-------|--------|----------|
317: | High | 3 | Tone matches — formality level, domain register, and audience consistent |
318: | Medium | 2 | Slight drift — somewhat more/less formal, but still appropriate for the context |
319: | Low | 1 | Noticeable mismatch — formal original made casual (or vice versa) without profile justification |
320: | Fail | 0 | Register violation — academic text made into slang, or casual text made into legalese |
321: 
322: **Profile exception:** When a profile explicitly shifts register (e.g., blog profile amplifies
323: informality), tone match is assessed against the *profile target*, not the original register.
324: 
325: ### 10.4 Length Ratio
326: 
327: Compares output length to original. Extreme changes suggest content loss or padding.
328: 
329: | Ratio | Points | Criteria |
330: |-------|--------|----------|
331: | 70–130% | 3 | Length preserved — natural variation within ±30% |
332: | 50–69% or 131–150% | 2 | Moderate change — some compression or expansion, likely acceptable |
333: | 30–49% or 151–200% | 1 | Significant change — substantial content probably lost or padded |
334: | < 30% or > 200% | 0 | Extreme change — content almost certainly lost or heavily padded |
335: 
336: **Calculation:** `length_ratio = len(output) / len(original) × 100`
337: 
338: Length is measured in characters (not words or tokens) for language-agnostic consistency.
339: 
340: ---
341: 
342: ## 11. Fidelity Severity Assignment Rubric
343: 
344: To reduce variance, apply these guidelines when scoring fidelity criteria:
345: 
346: ### Claims Preserved
347: - Count discrete factual claims in the original. If all appear (rephrased or not) → High.
348: - If only supporting details are dropped but the argument structure survives → Medium.
349: - If a numbered list loses items, a causal chain loses a step, or a key qualifier is dropped → Low.
350: 
351: ### No Fabrication
352: - Paraphrasing that changes word choice but not meaning → High.
353: - Adding a commonly-known context note ("Seoul, the capital of South Korea") → Medium.
354: - Inventing a statistic, date, or name not in the original → Low or Fail.
355: 
356: ### Tone Match
357: - Compare the first and last paragraphs of original vs. output for register cues.
358: - Profile-targeted register shifts are expected, not penalized.
359: - Mixed register (formal opening, casual middle) counts as Low.
360: 
361: ### Length Ratio
362: - This criterion is deterministic — compute the ratio and look up the table.
363: - No LLM judgment needed. Include the raw ratio in the score output.
364: 
365: ---
366: 
367: ## 12. Fidelity Scoring Formula
368: 
369: ### Per-Criterion Score
370: 
371: Each criterion is scored 0–3 (same as AI-likeness severity). The fidelity score normalizes
372: across all four criteria:
373: 
374: ```
375: fidelity_score = ((claims + fabrication + tone + length) / 12) × 100
376: ```
377: 
378: - Maximum: (3+3+3+3) / 12 × 100 = **100** (perfect fidelity)
379: - Minimum: (0+0+0+0) / 12 × 100 = **0** (total meaning loss)
380: 
381: ### Criterion Weighting (Optional)
382: 
383: For profiles that need non-uniform criterion importance, weights can be configured:
384: 
385: ```yaml
386: fidelity:
387:   weights:
388:     claims-preserved: 0.35
389:     no-fabrication: 0.30
390:     tone-match: 0.20
391:     length-ratio: 0.15
392: ```
393: 
394: When weights are configured:
395: 
396: ```
397: fidelity_score = Σ((criterion_points / 3) × criterion_weight) × 100
398: ```
399: 
400: Default weights (when not configured): equal at 0.25 each (equivalent to the simple formula).
401: 
402: ### Worked Example
403: 
404: Original: 4-paragraph academic article about climate change policy.
405: Output: Humanized version.
406: 
407: | Criterion | Score | Reasoning |
408: |-----------|-------|-----------|
409: | Claims preserved | 3 (High) | All policy recommendations and cited figures present |
410: | No fabrication | 2 (Medium) | Added "as widely reported" — minor inference stated as fact |
411: | Tone match | 3 (High) | Academic register maintained throughout |
412: | Length ratio | 2 (Moderate) | Output is 68% of original length (within 50-69% band) |
413: 
414: Fidelity = (3+2+3+2) / 12 × 100 = **83.3**
415: 
416: Interpretation: 76-90 range = "높은 충실도" (High fidelity, minor issues)
417: 
418: ### Fidelity Score Interpretation
419: 
420: | Range | Label | Meaning |
421: |-------|-------|---------|
422: | 91-100 | 완벽한 충실도 | Perfect fidelity — all meaning preserved |
423: | 76-90 | 높은 충실도 | High fidelity — minor omissions or additions |
424: | 51-75 | 보통 충실도 | Moderate fidelity — noticeable meaning changes |
425: | 26-50 | 낮은 충실도 | Low fidelity — significant meaning loss or fabrication |
426: | 0-25 | 의미 왜곡 | Meaning severely distorted or lost |
427: 
428: > **Note:** Unlike AI-likeness (lower = better), fidelity uses **higher = better**.
429: > A score of 83 means "roughly 73-93 range" given ±10 LLM variance —
430: > solidly in the "높은 충실도" band.
431: 
432: ---
433: 
434: ## 13. Combined Score
435: 
436: ### Formula
437: 
438: AI-likeness and fidelity compose into a combined score with configurable weighting:
439: 
440: ```
441: combined = (ai_likeness × ai_weight) + (fidelity_inverted × fidelity_weight)
442: ```
443: 
444: Where:
445: - `ai_likeness`: AI-likeness score from § 6 (0-100, lower = more human)
446: - `fidelity_inverted`: `100 - fidelity_score` (invert so both dimensions use "lower is better")
447: - `ai_weight + fidelity_weight = 1.0`
448: 
449: ### Default Weights
450: 
451: | Context | AI Weight | Fidelity Weight | Rationale |
452: |---------|-----------|-----------------|-----------|
453: | Default | 0.60 | 0.40 | Balanced — humanization is primary goal |
454: | Academic profile | 0.40 | 0.60 | Meaning preservation is critical in scholarly work |
455: | Blog profile | 0.70 | 0.30 | Creative rewriting tolerated |
456: | Technical profile | 0.35 | 0.65 | Accuracy is paramount in docs |
457: | Social profile | 0.75 | 0.25 | Tone transformation expected |
458: | Email profile | 0.50 | 0.50 | Equal importance |
459: | Legal profile | 0.35 | 0.65 | Legal precision must be preserved |
460: | Medical profile | 0.35 | 0.65 | Clinical accuracy is critical |
461: | Marketing profile | 0.65 | 0.35 | Tone transformation tolerated, creative rewriting expected |
462: | Namuwiki profile | 0.65 | 0.35 | Tone transformation tolerated for wiki-style cleanup |
463: 
464: Configurable via `ouroboros.combined-weights.{profile}` in `.patina.yaml`:
465: 
466: ```yaml
467: ouroboros:
468:   combined-weights:
469:     default:
470:       ai-likeness: 0.60
471:       fidelity: 0.40
472:     academic:
473:       ai-likeness: 0.40
474:       fidelity: 0.60
475: ```
476: 
477: ### Combined Score Interpretation
478: 
479: | Range | Label | Meaning |
480: |-------|-------|---------|
481: | 0-15 | 최적 | Excellent — human-like and faithful |
482: | 16-30 | 양호 | Good — minor issues in one or both dimensions |
483: | 31-50 | 보통 | Acceptable — noticeable trade-offs |
484: | 51-70 | 주의 | Caution — significant AI traces or meaning loss |
485: | 71-100 | 부적합 | Poor — heavy AI patterns and/or substantial meaning loss |
486: 
487: ### Ouroboros Termination
488: 
489: When used with `--ouroboros`, the loop terminates when:
490: - Combined score ≤ threshold (default: 30), OR
491: - Fidelity score drops below floor (default: 70) — **hard stop**, even if AI score improves
492: 
493: This prevents the ouroboros loop from "improving" AI score by destroying content.
494: 
495: ---
496: 
497: ## 14. MPS (Meaning Preservation Score) — Overview
498: 
499: AI-likeness (§§ 1–7) measures *how AI-like the output sounds*.
500: Fidelity (§§ 9–13) measures *how faithfully the output preserves overall meaning*.
501: MPS measures *whether specific semantic anchors survive the humanization pipeline*.
502: 
503: MPS is anchor-based: it tracks discrete meaning units (claims, polarity, causation, quantifiers, negations) extracted from the original text in SKILL.md Step 4.5, and checks whether each anchor is preserved after each pipeline phase.
504: 
505: MPS complements fidelity scoring — fidelity is a holistic LLM judgment, MPS is a structured anchor-by-anchor verification.
506: 
507: ---
508: 
509: ## 15. MPS Anchor Verification Criteria
510: 
511: Each anchor extracted in Step 4.5 is verified against the pipeline output. Verification produces one of three verdicts:
512: 
513: ### PASS
514: Anchor content is present in the output and its polarity is preserved. The anchor may be rephrased but its core assertion is unambiguously recoverable.
515: 
516: ### SOFT FAIL
517: Anchor content is present but weakened or made ambiguous. Examples:
518: - Specific claim became vague: "매출이 30% 증가" → "매출이 크게 증가"
519: - Quantifier lost precision: "p<0.05" → "통계적으로 유의미하다"
520: - Causal link became correlational: "A 때문에 B가 발생" → "A와 B는 관련이 있다"
521: - Definitive statement became hedged: "시스템이 실패했다" → "시스템에 문제가 있었을 수 있다"
522: 
523: ### HARD FAIL
524: Anchor content is deleted or its polarity is inverted. Examples:
525: - Claim removed entirely from output
526: - Negation dropped: "검증되지 않았다" → "검증되었다"
527: - Causation reversed: "A가 B를 야기했다" → "B가 A를 야기했다"
528: 
529: ### PASS vs SOFT FAIL Boundary
530: 
531: If a reader can **unambiguously recover** the original anchor's meaning from the rewritten version, it is PASS. If a reader could **reasonably interpret** the rewritten version differently from the original, it is SOFT FAIL.
532: 
533: ---
534: 
535: ## 16. MPS Scoring Formula
536: 
537: ### Base Formula
538: 
539: ```
540: anchor_pass_rate = PASS_count / total_anchor_count
541: polarity_preserved = polarity_PASS_count / total_polarity_anchor_count
542: 
543: MPS = (anchor_pass_rate × 0.6 + polarity_preserved × 0.4) × 100
544: ```
545: 
546: Where:
547: - `PASS_count`: anchors with PASS verdict after all remediation (including successful retries)
548: - `total_anchor_count`: all extracted anchors
549: - `polarity_PASS_count`: polarity-type anchors (Polarity + Negation) with PASS verdict
550: - `total_polarity_anchor_count`: all polarity-type anchors (Polarity + Negation)
551: 
552: ### Retry Counting Rule
553: 
554: If a SOFT FAIL anchor passes after alternative correction (retry), it counts as **PASS** in the formula. Only anchors that remain SOFT FAIL or HARD FAIL after all remediation are counted as failures.
555: 
556: ### Fallback (No Polarity Anchors)
557: 
558: If the text contains no Polarity or Negation anchors:
559: 
560: ```
561: MPS = anchor_pass_rate × 100
562: ```
563: 
564: ### Fallback (No Anchors Extracted)
565: 
566: When anchor extraction is skipped (text ≤1 paragraph and ≤2 sentences) or yields zero anchors:
567: 
568: ```
569: MPS = N/A (not applicable)
570: ```
571: 
572: When MPS = N/A:
573: - `--score` mode displays: `의미 보존 (MPS): N/A (앵커 없음)`
574: - Ouroboros loop: MPS floor는 우회되지 않는다. 결정론 루프의 `scoreMPS`는 숫자 mps를 반환하거나 스코어러 실패 시 `null`을 반환하며, `null`은 floor 위반으로 간주해 fail-closed로 직전 반복에 rollback한다 (아래 "Ouroboros Loop Gating" 및 `src/ouroboros.js` 참고). 앵커 N/A 표시는 SKILL 파이프라인 / `--score` 표시용이며 결정론 루프의 floor를 끄지 않는다.
575: 
576: ### MPS Interpretation
577: 
578: | Range | Label | Meaning |
579: |-------|-------|---------|
580: | 90–100 | 우수 (Excellent) | Full meaning preservation |
581: | 70–89 | 양호 (Good) | Minor weakening, acceptable |
582: | 50–69 | 주의 (Warning) | Significant anchor loss, review needed |
583: | < 50 | 위험 (Critical) | Severe meaning corruption |
584: 
585: ### Worked Example
586: 
587: Original text (3 paragraphs) with extracted anchors:
588: 
589: | # | Type | Content | Verdict | After Retry |
590: |---|------|---------|---------|-------------|
591: | 1 | Claim | "시스템이 실패했다" | SOFT FAIL | PASS (retry succeeded) |
592: | 2 | Polarity | "아직 검증되지 않았다" (negative) | PASS | — |
593: | 3 | Quantifier | "매출 30% 증가" | PASS | — |
594: | 4 | Causation | "A 때문에 B 발생" | HARD FAIL | — (original restored) |
595: | 5 | Negation | "불가능하다" | PASS | — |
596: 
597: After remediation:
598: - PASS: #1 (retry), #2, #3, #5 = 4
599: - HARD FAIL: #4 = 1 (original restored, so meaning is preserved in output but pattern not humanized)
600: - Total anchors: 5
601: - Polarity anchors (#2, #5): both PASS = 2/2
602: 
603: ```
604: anchor_pass_rate = 4/5 = 0.80
605: polarity_preserved = 2/2 = 1.00
606: MPS = (0.80 × 0.6 + 1.00 × 0.4) × 100 = (0.48 + 0.40) × 100 = 88
607: ```
608: 
609: Interpretation: 70–89 range = "양호" (Good, minor weakening)
610: 
611: > **Note:** Anchor #4 was a HARD FAIL, so its original sentence was restored in the output.
612: > The anchor counts as a failure in MPS (reducing the score), but the meaning IS preserved
613: > in the output because the original was kept. MPS reflects humanization success rate,
614: > not output meaning accuracy (which is always preserved via fallback).
615: 
616: ### MPS vs Fidelity: Complementary Metrics
617: 
618: MPS measures **humanization coverage** — what fraction of meaning anchors were successfully humanized while being preserved. A HARD FAIL anchor that was restored to its original wording counts as a humanization failure (the pattern wasn't removable without meaning loss), even though the final output's meaning is intact. Fidelity (§§ 9-13) measures **overall output meaning accuracy** against the original — restored sentences score perfectly on fidelity. Use both metrics together: high fidelity + low MPS means "meaning is safe but some AI patterns couldn't be removed."
619: 
620: ---
621: 
622: ## 17. MPS Integration Points
623: 
624: ### `--score` Mode Output
625: 
626: When `--score` is used with rewrite or ouroboros mode (original text available),
627: MPS is displayed alongside AI-likeness and Fidelity:
628: 
629: | 지표 | 점수 |
630: |------|------|
631: | AI 유사도 | 23/100 (낮을수록 좋음) |
632: | 충실도 | 87/100 (높을수록 좋음) |
633: | 의미 보존 (MPS) | 92/100 (높을수록 좋음) |
634: | 종합 | 25/100 (낮을수록 좋음) |
635: 
636: > **Note:** MPS is NOT included in the combined score formula (§13).
637: > Combined score uses fidelity (holistic) while MPS is a structural verification metric.
638: > Both are displayed for transparency but serve different purposes.
639: 
640: ### Ouroboros Loop Gating
641: 
642: MPS floor = 70 (default). Independent of fidelity floor.
643: 
644: Termination condition:
645: - MPS < mps-floor → terminate with reason: **의미 보존 하한 위반** → rollback to previous iteration
646: 
647: Both fidelity floor AND MPS floor must pass for an iteration to be accepted.
648: 
649: Configurable via `.patina.yaml`:
650: 
651: ```yaml
652: ouroboros:
653:   mps-floor: 70  # default
654: ```
````

## File: patterns/en-language.md
````markdown
  1: ---
  2: pack: en-language
  3: language: en
  4: name: Language Patterns
  5: version: 1.1.0
  6: patterns: 9
  7: corpus-snapshot:
  8:   id: bootstrap-patterns-pre-provenance
  9:   status: needs-quarterly-refresh
 10:   source: maintainer-curated pattern packs before quarterly corpus snapshot tracking
 11:   last_validated: null
 12: dedupe-with:
 13:   - source: "en-language:7"
 14:     target: "en-filler:24"
 15:     owner: "en-filler:24"
 16:     reason: "The phrase 'stands as a testament' belongs to filler #24; language #7 keeps 'testament' only as one word in a broader AI-vocabulary cluster."
 17:   - source: "en-language:7"
 18:     target: "en-language:8"
 19:     owner: "en-language:8"
 20:     reason: "When 'testament' appears inside a copula-avoidance construction, pattern #8 owns the rewrite; pattern #7 only fires on a broader AI-vocabulary cluster."
 21:   - source: "en-language:8"
 22:     target: "en-filler:24"
 23:     owner: "en-filler:24"
 24:     reason: "The exact phrase 'stands as a testament' is a generic positive-conclusion marker when used as vague praise; pattern #8 still owns non-conclusion copula avoidance such as 'serves as'."
 25: ---
 26: 
 27: # Language Patterns
 28: 
 29: ### 7. AI Vocabulary Words
 30: 
 31: **Watch words:** delve, tapestry, landscape, multifaceted, comprehensive, pivotal, testament, intricate, nuanced, leverage, foster, crucial, moreover, furthermore, realm, robust, facilitate, endeavor, resonate, underscore, embark, myriad, paramount, encompass, holistic, synergy, elucidate, culminate, juxtapose, burgeoning
 32: 
 33: **Fire condition:** 3+ watch words appear in one paragraph.
 34: 
 35: **Semantic Risk:** MEDIUM
 36: **Preservation Note:** Replacing AI vocabulary words changes the specific connotations carried by words like "nuanced" or "multifaceted"; ensure the substituted terms preserve the intended meaning and register.
 37: 
 38: **Problem:** These words appear at dramatically higher rates in AI-generated text than in human writing. Individually they are normal English words; clustered together they are a telltale fingerprint.
 39: 
 40: **Before:**
 41: > This comprehensive report delves into the multifaceted landscape of renewable energy, leveraging nuanced insights to foster a robust understanding. Moreover, the intricate tapestry of stakeholder interests underscores the pivotal role of policy in this crucial endeavor.
 42: 
 43: **After:**
 44: > This report covers renewable energy policy from three angles: cost, grid reliability, and public opinion. Each section uses data from the EIA and interviews with six state utility commissioners.
 45: 
 46: ---
 47: 
 48: ### 8. Copula Avoidance ("serves as")
 49: 
 50: **Watch words:** serves as, acts as, functions as, stands as, operates as, works as, remains as, exists as
 51: 
 52: **Fire condition:** 2+ copula-avoidance constructions in the same paragraph, or a single instance where "is" would be shorter and clearer.
 53: 
 54: **Exclusion:** "Serves as" is acceptable when the subject has a formally designated role function (e.g., "the committee serves as an advisory board" — institutional role, not decorative usage).
 55: 
 56: **Semantic Risk:** MEDIUM
 57: **Preservation Note:** "Serves as" sometimes implies a functional or designated role distinct from mere identity; replacing with "is" may flatten a meaningful distinction about purpose or function.
 58: 
 59: **Problem:** AI avoids the simple verb "is" by using unnecessarily complex copula constructions. "The park serves as a gathering place" instead of "The park is a gathering place."
 60: 
 61: **Before:**
 62: > The library serves as a vital community hub, functioning as both an educational resource and a social gathering space. It also acts as a testament to the city's commitment to public access.
 63: 
 64: **After:**
 65: > The library is the neighborhood's main public space. People use it for study groups, ESL classes, and the Saturday morning story hour, which draws about 40 kids a week.
 66: 
 67: ---
 68: 
 69: ### 9. Negative Parallelisms
 70: 
 71: **Watch words:** not just...but, not merely...but also, not only...but, goes beyond...to, more than just...it is, transcends...to become, serial negation ("It wasn't X. It wasn't Y. It was Z."), "Not a X. Not a Y. A Z."
 72: 
 73: **Fire condition:** 2+ "not X but Y" structures in the same document, or a single instance where the positive statement alone would be simpler and equally clear. Also fires on a single serial-negation build-up (two or more consecutive "it wasn't/is not X" sentences resolving into "it was/is Z") — the negations are a runway, not content.
 74: 
 75: **Exclusion:** Genuine contrastive clarification correcting a misconception ("the event is not a conference but a workshop — no keynotes, only hands-on sessions") — the negative frame is doing real work here.
 76: 
 77: **Semantic Risk:** MEDIUM
 78: **Preservation Note:** The negative framing sometimes carries a genuine contrast or correction; collapsing "not X but Y" to just "Y" may lose the implicit rejection of a common assumption the author intended to address.
 79: 
 80: **Problem:** AI uses negative-then-positive constructions to make simple points sound profound. Instead of stating what something is, it first states what it is not.
 81: 
 82: **Before:**
 83: > This initiative is not just a policy change but a fundamental reimagining of urban planning. It goes beyond mere infrastructure investment to become a statement about the kind of city residents want to live in.
 84: 
 85: **After:**
 86: > The initiative rezones 12 blocks for mixed-use development and adds a bike lane network connecting the east and west sides of the river.
 87: 
 88: ---
 89: 
 90: ### 10. Rule of Three Overuse
 91: 
 92: **Fire condition:** 3-item lists appear 2+ times in the same document, or a triple-part sentence where the count is arbitrary and another count would be equally valid.
 93: 
 94: **Exclusion:** Naturally occurring triads in genuinely three-part processes (past/present/future; input/process/output; beginning/middle/end) are not this pattern.
 95: 
 96: **Burstiness note:** When rewriting, use the naturally correct count — one strong point if that is all there is, two contrasting items if that is the real structure, four if there are actually four. Varying list counts across a document signals human authorship.
 97: 
 98: **Problem:** AI defaults to triple-item lists and three-part structures when the natural count might be two, four, or one. This creates a rhythm that feels rehearsed rather than spontaneous.
 99: 
100: **Semantic Risk:** LOW
101: 
102: **Before:**
103: > The program fosters creativity, innovation, and collaboration. Participants gain inspiration, practical skills, and lasting connections. The result is a more dynamic, inclusive, and forward-thinking community.
104: 
105: **After:**
106: > The program pairs early-career designers with experienced mentors for a 10-week project. Last year's cohort produced two apps that are still in active use.
107: 
108: ---
109: 
110: ### 11. Elegant Variation (Synonym Cycling)
111: 
112: **Fire condition:** The same entity referred to by 3+ different names or synonyms within a single paragraph.
113: 
114: **Exclusion:** Legitimate disambiguation (using "the company" vs. "Microsoft" when distinguishing a parent from a subsidiary, or "the study" vs. "the 2023 Stanford paper" for precision) is not this pattern.
115: 
116: **Problem:** AI rotates synonyms for the same entity to avoid repeating a word, producing text that feels strangely evasive. A city becomes "the metropolis," then "the urban center," then "the municipality" — all within the same paragraph.
117: 
118: **Semantic Risk:** LOW
119: 
120: **Before:**
121: > Tokyo is the most populous city in Japan. The metropolis is known for its blend of tradition and modernity. The urban center attracts millions of tourists each year. The Japanese capital continues to grow.
122: 
123: **After:**
124: > Tokyo is the most populous city in Japan, with about 14 million residents. It draws roughly 20 million foreign tourists a year, a number that has doubled since 2015.
125: 
126: ---
127: 
128: ### 12. False Ranges
129: 
130: **Watch words:** from X to Y, ranging from...to, spanning...to, everything from...to, whether...or
131: 
132: **Fire condition:** A "from X to Y" construction appears where the two poles do not meaningfully bound a spectrum — the range is decorative rather than informative.
133: 
134: **Exclusion:** Genuine numeric or temporal ranges ("from 10 to 100 employees", "from January to March", "from $5 to $50") are not this pattern.
135: 
136: **Problem:** AI creates artificial ranges to sound comprehensive, often pairing two extremes that do not meaningfully define a spectrum.
137: 
138: **Semantic Risk:** MEDIUM
139: **Preservation Note:** Removing a range construction may drop a genuine scope claim about audience or coverage; verify whether the range was intended to convey actual breadth before eliminating it.
140: 
141: **Before:**
142: > The festival offers something for everyone, from young children to seasoned professionals, spanning everything from traditional folk music to cutting-edge electronic performances.
143: 
144: **After:**
145: > The festival has three stages. The main stage books established acts (this year: Yo-Yo Ma, Thundercat). The tent stage is for local bands. There is also a children's area with instrument demos.
146: 
147: ---
148: 
149: ### 32. Comparison Adverb Overuse ("more" without target)
150: 
151: **Watch words:** more specific, more concrete, more efficient, more effective, more comprehensive, more robust, more seamless, more meaningful, more strategic, more impactful, more nuanced, more proactive, more sustainable, more scalable
152: 
153: **Fire condition:** 2+ "more + adjective/adverb" comparative phrases appear in one document without a clear target, baseline, or metric. A single instance can fire when it appears in the same paragraph as other formal AI markers such as "comprehensive", "strategic", "framework", "stakeholder", or "in-depth".
154: 
155: **Exclusion:** Do not fire when the comparison target is explicit ("more efficient than the old process"), when a before/after metric is provided ("reduced latency from 240 ms to 130 ms"), in ordinary quantifiers ("more than 10 users"), or in fixed phrases such as "more or less".
156: 
157: **Semantic Risk:** LOW
158: **Preservation Note:** Comparative language can carry a real improvement claim. Preserve the target, metric, or priority if one exists; otherwise replace the vague comparative with the concrete requirement or result the sentence is trying to name.
159: 
160: **Problem:** AI text often implies progress by stacking "more X" phrases while never saying compared with what. Human prose either names the baseline ("faster than last quarter's build") or drops the decorative comparison and states the concrete change.
161: 
162: **Burstiness note:** Do not convert every "more X" phrase into the same alternative. Mix direct adjectives, named metrics, shorter verbs, and one retained comparative when the contrast is real.
163: 
164: **Before:**
165: > The initiative will enable more specific milestones, more efficient resource allocation, and more comprehensive stakeholder alignment. Moving forward, the team should develop a more strategic framework for more meaningful collaboration.
166: 
167: **After:**
168: > The initiative needs dated milestones, named owners, and a budget review. The team also needs one shared plan for partner handoffs, because the current spreadsheet has three conflicting owners for the same launch tasks.
169: 
170: ---
171: 
172: ### 33. Definitional-Metaphor Equation ("X is the architecture of Z")
173: 
174: **Watch words:** is the signature of, is the shape of, is the language of, is the currency of, is the architecture of, is the backbone of, is the engine of, is the heartbeat of, is the DNA of, is the cornerstone of, is the lifeblood of
175: 
176: **Fire condition:** 2+ copula sentences of the form "X is the [abstract noun] of Z" appear in the same document/section without concrete support for the equation. A single instance is an audit hint only: do not rewrite unless the same inflated metaphor pattern recurs.
177: 
178: **Exclusion:** Literal or technical definitions ("water is the universal solvent"), established idioms and textbook metaphors ("the mitochondria is the powerhouse of the cell"), factual "X is the capital/center of Z" statements, and genuine equivalences backed by concrete support in the same passage are not this pattern.
179: 
180: **Disambiguation from #8 (Copula Avoidance):** #8 fires on text that *avoids* "is" ("serves as", "functions as" → rewrite to "is"). #33 is the opposite: the sentence already uses "is", but inflates it into an "is the [abstract noun] of [abstraction]" metaphor-equation to manufacture profundity. Do not conflate them — #8 wants the copula restored, #33 wants the empty metaphor-equation dismantled.
181: 
182: **Semantic Risk:** MEDIUM
183: **Preservation Note:** The metaphor sometimes points at a real claim ("trust depends on consistent behavior"); when rewriting, recover and state that underlying claim concretely rather than deleting it, so a genuine point is not lost along with the inflated framing.
184: 
185: **Problem:** AI manufactures depth by equating one abstraction with another through a borrowed structural noun — "signature", "architecture", "currency". The sentence sounds like an insight but asserts nothing testable; swapping the abstract noun ("is the *language* of" → "is the *currency* of") barely changes the meaning, which exposes the equation as decorative rather than substantive.
186: 
187: **Before:**
188: > Symmetry is the architecture of trust. Cringe is the visible signature of moving along a gradient you chose. Consistency is the currency of every relationship that lasts.
189: 
190: **After:**
191: > People trust a process more when it behaves the same way every time, so they can predict it. That predictability is what makes a relationship hold up: you keep doing what you said you would, and the other side stops bracing for surprises.
192: 
193: ### 34. False Agency (Inanimate Actors)
194: 
195: **Watch words:** the data tells us, the numbers speak, the decision emerged, the culture shifted, the conversation moved toward, the market rewards, the market decides, the complaint becomes, the strategy demands, this unlocks, the moment calls for, the work speaks for itself
196: 
197: **Fire condition:** 2+ sentences in the same document where an inanimate subject performs a human action (deciding, telling, rewarding, demanding, moving a discussion) *and* the human actor is recoverable from context but never named. A single instance is an audit hint only.
198: 
199: **Exclusion:**
200: - Academic/report citation conventions ("the study finds", "the paper argues", "the data show a correlation") — standard metonymy for the authors.
201: - Genuine agentless processes ("the temperature rose", "the deadline passed", "the market crashed" as an aggregate event).
202: - Deliberate literary personification sustained as a device, and established idioms.
203: - Software/system subjects doing what software actually does ("the linter flags", "the pipeline retries").
204: 
205: **Disambiguation from #26/#27 (Passive/Nominalization):** passive voice hides the actor by grammar ("the decision was made"); false agency hides the actor by promoting an object to actor ("the decision emerged"). Both leave the same question — *who did it* — but this pattern fires on active-voice sentences, which passive detectors miss.
206: 
207: **Semantic Risk:** HIGH
208: **Preservation Note:** Never invent an actor the source does not support. If the text implies the actor (a team, the author, "you"), name that; if no actor is recoverable, restructure around what is actually known ("someone on the team killed the project" → only if the source says so; otherwise "the project was killed within days" may have to survive as-is). Fabricating agency is a meaning violation, not a style fix.
209: 
210: **Problem:** AI systematically promotes abstractions into actors — decisions emerge, cultures shift, data tells, markets reward — because it lets the prose sound analytical while never committing to who did what. Human writing tends to name the person, or at least stand somewhere ("we", "you", "the team"). The tell is not one sentence but the accumulation: a paragraph where every event happens with no human in it.
211: 
212: **Before:**
213: > The feedback loop rewarded speed over quality, so the culture shifted within a quarter. Decisions emerged from Slack threads rather than meetings, and the roadmap bent toward whatever the metrics demanded.
214: 
215: **After:**
216: > Leadership paid out bonuses on shipping speed, not defect counts, so within a quarter engineers stopped polishing. People started making calls in Slack threads instead of meetings, and the PMs rewrote the roadmap around whatever number the dashboard showed that week.
````

  41: /**
  42:  * Structural classifier score is a calibrated probability-like document signal.
